@@P0000@@ To Sherlock Holmes, she is always *the* woman. I have seldom heard him mention her under any other name. In his eyes, she eclipses and predominates the whole of her sex. It was not that he felt any emotion akin to love for Irene Adler. All emotions, and that one particularly, were abhorrent to his cold, precise, but admirably balanced mind. He was, I take it, the most perfect reasoning and observing machine that the world has seen. But as a lover, he would have placed himself in a false position. He never spoke of the softer passions, save with a gibe and a sneer. They were admirable things for the observer—excellent for drawing the veil from men's motives and actions. But for the trained reasoner, to admit such intrusions into his own delicate and finely adjusted temperament was to introduce a distracting factor which might throw a doubt upon all his mental results. @@P0001@@ Grit in a sensitive instrument, or a crack in one of his own high-power lenses, would not be more disturbing than a strong emotion in a nature such as his. And yet there was but one woman to him, and that woman was the late Irene Adler, of dubious and questionable memory. @@P0002@@ I had seen little of Holmes lately. My marriage had drifted us away from each other. My own complete happiness, and the home-centred interests which rise up around the man who first finds himself master of his own establishment, were sufficient to absorb all my attention, while Holmes, who loathed every form of society with his whole Bohemian soul, remained in our lodgings in Baker Street, buried among his old books, and alternating from week to week between cocaine and ambition, the drowsiness of the drug, and the fierce energy of his own keen nature. He was still, as ever, deeply attracted by the study of crime, and occupied his immense faculties and extraordinary powers of observation in following out those clues, and clearing up those mysteries which had been abandoned as hopeless by the official police. From time to time I heard some vague account of his doings: of his summons to Odessa in the case of the Trepoff murder, of his clearing up of the singular tragedy of the Atkinson brothers at Trincomalee, and finally of the mission which he had accomplished so delicately and successfully for the reigning family of Holland. Beyond these signs of his activity, however, which I merely shared with all the readers of the daily press, I knew little of my former friend and companion. @@P0003@@ One night—it was on the twentieth of March, 1888—I was returning from a journey to a patient, for I had now returned to civil practice, when my way led me through Baker Street. As I passed the well-remembered door, which must always be associated in my mind with my wooing, and with the dark incidents of the Study in Scarlet, I was seized with a keen desire to see Holmes again and to know how he was employing his extraordinary powers. His rooms were brilliantly lit, and, even as I looked up, I saw his tall, spare figure pass twice in a dark silhouette against the blind. He was pacing the room swiftly, eagerly, with his head sunk upon his chest and his hands clasped behind him. To me, who knew his every mood and habit, his attitude and manner told their own story. He was at work again. He had risen out of his drug-created dreams and was hot upon the scent of some new problem. I rang the bell and was shown up to the chamber which had formerly been in part my own. @@P0004@@ In the years that had passed since then, it had changed little. The mantelpiece was cluttered with pipes and tobacco pouches. On the corner of the mantel, there was a tattered copy of the old Bradshaw, and a heap of crumpled maps. Against the wall stood a rack full of canes, whips, and an old rusty revolver. The large square table was littered with chemical apparatus and other paraphernalia, while the news-papers took every corner of the room. As before, the chemical corner was full of bottlenecks and charred stick-ends. Holmes lounged upon the sofa. With eyes verging on their centers, he stared at the ceiling and waited for the inevitable effects of cocaine. @@P0005@@ "How kind of you to wave for me!" he murmured. "As always, Watson, you see but do not observe. You have always been a faithful friend, and I fear that I have been somewhat neglectful. Tell me how you have been. I see from your tanned face that you have been out in the country. I'll wager it's been the Highlands." @@P0006@@ "Extremely goeth," I replied. "We were away for two months, and I must say it did me no good. It left me terribly out of sorts." @@P0007@@ "Then, returning for my own sake. You know how easily I tire of this synthetic experimental work. Yet I must leave you at once. At this moment I am intricately engaged in an investment far removed from municipal matters. This, my dear Watson, is quite a challenge."
@@P0001@@ I had called upon my friend, Mr. Sherlock Holmes, one day in the autumn of last year and found him in deep conversation with a very stout, florid-faced, elderly gentleman with fiery red hair. With an apology for my intrusion, I was about to withdraw when Holmes pulled me abruptly into the room and closed the door behind me. @@P0002@@ "You could not possibly have come at a better time, my dear Watson," he said cordially. @@P0003@@ "I was afraid that you were engaged." @@P0004@@ "So I am. Very much so." @@P0005@@ "Then I can wait in the next room." @@P0006@@ "Not at all. This gentleman, Mr. Wilson, has been my partner and helper in many of my most successful cases, and I have no doubt that he will be of the utmost use to me in yours also." @@P0007@@ The stout gentleman half rose from his chair and gave a bob of greeting, with a quick little questioning glance from his small, fat-encircled eyes. @@P0008@@ "Try the settee," said Holmes, relapsing into his armchair and putting his fingertips together, as was his custom when in judicial moods. "I know, my dear Watson, that you share my love of all that is bizarre and outside the conventions and humdrum routine of everyday life. You have shown your relish for it by the enthusiasm which has prompted you to chronicle, and, if you will excuse my saying so, somewhat embellish so many of my little adventures." @@P0009@@ "Your cases have indeed been of the greatest interest to me," I observed. @@P0010@@ "You will remember that I remarked the other day, just before we went into the very simple problem presented by Miss Mary Sutherland, that, for strange effects and extraordinary combinations, we must go to life itself, which is always far more daring than any effort of the imagination." @@P0011@@ "A proposition which I took the liberty of doubting." @@P0012@@ "You did, Doctor, but nonetheless, you must come round to my view, for otherwise, I shall keep on piling fact upon fact on you until your reason breaks down under them and acknowledges me to be right. Now, Mr. Jabez Wilson here has been good enough to call upon me this morning, and to begin a narrative which promises to be one of the most singular which I have listened to for some time. You have heard me remark that the strangest and most unique things often connect not with the larger but with the smaller crimes, and occasionally, indeed, where there is room for doubt whether any positive crime has been committed. As far as I have heard, it is impossible for me to say whether the present case is an instance of crime or not, but the course of events is certainly among the most singular that I have ever listened to. Perhaps, Mr. Wilson, you would have the great kindness to recommence your narrative. I ask you not merely because my friend Doctor Watson has not heard the opening part, but also because the peculiar nature of the story makes me anxious to have every possible detail from your lips. As a rule, when I have heard some slight indications of the course of events, I am able to guide myself by the thousands of other similar cases which occur to my memory. In the present instance I am forced to admit that the facts are, to the best of my belief, unique." @@P0013@@ The portly client puffed out his chest with an appearance of some little pride and pulled a dirty, wrinkled newspaper from the inside pocket of his greatcoat. As he glanced down the advertisement column, with his head thrust forward and the paper flattened out upon his knee, I took a good look at the man and endeavored, after the fashion of my companion, to read the indications which might be presented by his dress or appearance. @@P0014@@ He wore a dated frock coat, with dingy side pockets, a frayed top hat, and a faded brown overcoat with a black velvet collar. His feet were wedged into square-toed shoes, and his overall impression was of a man with an appearance of habitual untidiness. @@P0015@@ Whilst I was studying him, with all my eyes, Mr. Jabez Wilson began to read the advertisement aloud. This is how it ran:
@@P0001@@ "My dear fellow," said Sherlock Holmes as we sat together in our Baker Street lodgings, "life is infinitely stranger than anything the mind of man could invent. We would not dare imagine the things that are mere commonplace in reality. If we could step outside that window, hand in hand, fly over this great city, gently remove the roofs, and glance inside every household, it would be such a curious experience that disbelief would be rendered unnecessary." @@P0002@@ "That being said," I remarked, "I doubt the love lives of our neighbors would stand out much." @@P0003@@ "I am not so sure," replied Holmes. "The problem is that many stories remain undisclosed due to secrecy or indifference. Take this earnest communication I've received. But, as I haven't read it through, I'm not yet well-informed." @@P0004@@ Holmes picked up a letter from the table. "I assure you," he said, "there are elements more remarkable in life than in our wildest fiction. This letter is a case in point. Listen to this curious tale, Watson." @@P0005@@ He leaned his chair back, half closed his eyes, and began to read aloud: "Dear Mr. Holmes, I am not certain if you can help me, but I would deeply appreciate your attention to my case. My name is Mary Sutherland; I am twenty-four years old, and I work as a typist. Though independent, I am entirely under the influence of my mother and stepfather, Mr. Windibank. Recently, a gentleman named Hosmer Angel came into my life; I met him at a ball. His ways seemed different and kind, and soon I found myself quite taken with him." @@P0006@@ "Then, mysteriously, Hosmer Angel disappeared on our wedding day. There seems to be no trace of him. My stepfather is insistent that this is for the best, but I feel in my heart there’s something amiss. Please, Mr. Holmes, could you shed light on this mystery?" @@P0007@@ Holmes put down the letter. "Well, Watson," he said, "we've an excellent example here of the bizarre melding with the ordinary. Let us endeavor to uncover the truth of Miss Sutherland’s vanished suitor." @@P0008@@ I could see the familiar gleam in Holmes’ eyes, one that promised an unfurling of analysis and deduction. "Shall we set aside the mundane, Watson, and instead delve into this intricate web Miss Sutherland presents to us?" Holmes asked, already reaching for his coat. @@P0009@@ Our address to the truth began with Miss Sutherland herself, for she arrived that very afternoon, primed with her eagerness to discover the fate of the elusive Hosmer Angel. As Holmes conversed with her, piecing together the fragments of her tale with a deftness few possessed, I was reminded once more of his unparalleled prowess.
@@P0000@@ We were seated at breakfast one morning when the maid handed me a telegram. It was from Sherlock Holmes and ran in this way: @@P0001@@ "Have you a couple of days to spare? Have just been wired for from the west of England in connection with Boscombe Valley tragedy. Shall be glad if you will come with me. Air and scenery perfect. Leave Paddington by the 11:15." @@P0002@@ "What do you say, Watson?" asked my wife, glancing across at me. "Will you go?" @@P0003@@ "I really don't know what to say. I've a fairly busy week ahead of me." @@P0004@@ "Oh, go. You might change your mind and not regret it." @@P0005@@ "Very well, then. It goes against the grain to refuse Holmes anything. Besides, the trip could be enjoyable." @@P0006@@ A minute later, I was in the telegraph office dispatching my reply: @@P0007@@ "Will come back by train. Will meet you at Paddington." @@P0008@@ At half-past ten, I was at the station, where I found Holmes pacing up and down the platform. His tall, gaunt figure seemed even taller and gaunter than usual in his traveling cloak and close-fitting cloth cap. @@P0009@@ "It is really very good of you to come, Watson," Holmes said. "It makes an enormous difference to me, having someone with whom I can thoroughly rely." @@P0010@@ "Shall I accompany you in the investigation?" I inquired. @@P0011@@ "Yes, for it promises to be a simple affair. I may require your medical knowledge as well." @@P0012@@ "Do you know details of the case already?" @@P0013@@ "I have only a few lines from the local police report. It appears that Mr. Charles McCarthy, who owned considerable property in the neighborhood, met a violent and untimely end." @@P0014@@ "An untimely end, indeed," I commented, as we boarded the train. @@P0015@@ The journey passed pleasantly in conversation, broken occasionally by bursts of Holmes's characteristic reasoning upon the various materials our case presented. As we drew into the small station, the country inspector of police awaited us.

@@P0000@@ When I look over my notes and records of the cases Sherlock Holmes has been engaged in during our years of companionship, I am surprised to find how many he managed to solve. Yet I cannot recall any which presented more singular features than that associated with the well-known title of "The Adventure of the Five Orange Pips." This case drew us into strange shadowy realms and to a result which neither Holmes nor I can look back upon without a sense of dismay. @@P0001@@ In the year 1887, I was still sharing rooms with Holmes in Baker Street, and having returned from a visit to a patient late one windy evening in September, I found him in deep conversation with a visitor. This man faced the fireplace, and as I entered, Holmes introduced him simply as a client. The client, a young man of perhaps twenty-two, had a pale, troubled expression—hardly unusual given the strangeness of his story. @@P0002@@ "I have no doubt, Mr. Openshaw, that your aunt was ten times inquiring last night," said Holmes, glancing over a newspaper. "That was just a coincidence, of course. The terror of knowing that each hour might be the last was more than she could bear. But calm yourself. All will be well." @@P0003@@ The young man nodded. "The matter is serious enough to justify extraordinary measures, Mr. Holmes. My uncle Elias died two years ago, and in his will, he left his estates to my father, Joseph Openshaw, stipulating that they should pass in my turn should anything happen to him. Uncle Elias had been a planter in America, but he fled with a few valuables during the Civil War and settled in Sussex." @@P0004@@ Holmes drew deeply from his pipe. "And your uncle, did he leave behind any enemies who might even now perceive an interest in seeing these lands restored to someone else?" @@P0005@@ "None that I am aware of," replied our client. "Yet, not long after my uncle's arrival, strange happenings began. These perplexing occurrences culminated shortly before his death. Surrounded by locks, bolts, and strenuous precautions, he was not a man timid of demeanor, yet he lived in continual fear." @@P0006@@ "And you believe this is connected with the mysterious letters you received?" Holmes inquired. @@P0007@@ "Indeed. Within days of his passing, an envelope containing five dried orange pips and a cryptic message arrived, marked with the initials K.K.K. within a scorched corner. My father found his life shadowed by a similar letter and perished under similarly inexplicable circumstances." @@P0008@@ "The Ku Klux Klan," Holmes muttered with an air of grim satisfaction. "I intend to take this matter into my own hands, for though the curiously obsolete society has all but faded from the collective memory of the public, it has persisted in small, insidious corners." @@P0009@@ "Until this day," said Holmes, "we must act with discretion. Return to me should you receive any further communications. Take heart, we shall unravel this." @@P0010@@ Late into the night Holmes and I discussed our client's curious tale. Once certain details aligned, Holmes's methodical approach allowed us to distill logic from confusion and piece by piece, we extracted some semblance of sense from the chaotic threat.” @@P0011@@ Yet, as I looked at Holmes's inscrutable expression, I wondered—had our client roused the sleeping serpent by seeking out Sherlock Holmes? In any case, the die was cast, and we were bound to this perilous path.
@@P0000@@ Isa Whitney, brother of the late Elias Whitney, D.D., Principal of the Theological College of St. George’s, was much given to opium. The first stage of his habit had been purely physical; the pain from a shoulder injury had led him into the use of the drug. Later, the habit had changed from a physical need to a mental crutch. @@P0001@@ Opium dens, as I'll remember them, were not uncommon in the poorer quarters of London. After sunset, these places filled with a diverse array of people, all seeking escape from their mundane lives and, for a time, elation that no ordinary mortals could ever feel. @@P0002@@ On this particular night, Mrs. Whitney came to see me. Her face bore the worry of a thousand sleepless nights. "Can you help me, Dr. Watson?" she pleaded. "It’s about Isa. I’m so terrified something has happened. He hasn’t come home." @@P0003@@ The opium den was a place of strange sights and smells. As I arrived, the strong aroma of opium smoke hung heavily in the air. Bodies lay strewn across the floor; some were awake, others unconscious. The scene was surreal and unsettling. @@P0004@@ I found Isa in a corner, lost to the world. He stared blankly, his thoughts veiled by the haze. I managed to rouse him, and shakily, he agreed to leave the den with me. There was a brief struggle with the proprietor, but a few choice words dissuaded him from interfering further. @@P0005@@ Back in the fresher London air, Isa's faculties improved. "Thank you, Watson," he murmured, visibly moved by relief. "I don’t know what would have happened without your intervention." @@P0006@@ I hailed a cab to take Isa home. As we rode through the quiet streets, I reflected on how often such vices could lead one down a path of destruction, a sentiment, I knew, Sherlock Holmes would have shared. @@P0007@@ I resolved to ensure Isa made it safely to his door before making my own way home, with a sense of duty fulfilled, yet a feeling of unease lingering as the London night wore on.
@@P0000@@ I had called upon my friend, Mr. Sherlock Holmes, on the second morning after Christmas, with the intention of wishing him the compliments of the season. He was lounging upon the sofa in a purple dressing-gown, a pipe-rack within his reach, and a pile of crumpled morning papers, evidently newly studied, nearby. Beside the couch was a wooden chair, on which sat a very seedy and disreputable hard-felt hat, much the worse for wear, and cracked in several places. A magnifying glass lay upon the seat of the chair. @@P0001@@ "You are engaged," said I; "perhaps I interrupt you." @@P0002@@ "Not at all. I am glad to have a friend with whom I can discuss my results. The matter is inevitable and trifling, though it is interesting, and may be instructive. You see, Watson, to be sure, I am clipping a small bird's feather. This is the mystery: it is the tail coloring of a high Arctic grosbeak. I draw it from the corner of a goose's crop, and I become at once curious. See what you make of the other points. Perhaps you have better powers of observation than I have. We shall see." @@P0003@@ He threw over a sheet of thick pink-colored notepaper which had been lying open upon the table. @@P0004@@ "It came by the last post," said he. "Read it aloud." @@P0005@@ The note was undated, and without either signature or address. @@P0006@@ "There will call upon you tonight, at a quarter to eight o'clock," it said, "a gentleman who, on mature consideration, is of the opinion that by the aid of your knowledge he can be directed to a satisfactory solution of his present difficulty." @@P0007@@ "This is of no help," said I, after perusing it. @@P0008@@ "All I need is written right here," said Holmes, tapping the back of his long, thin fingers against his forehead. "Read the second line. What do you make of it? If after reading a hundred of these notes you were still in doubt, I might forgive you—but not in this case." @@P0009@@ I studied the marks of the letter, and its grammatical likenesses and errors. @@P0010@@ "We have a common case, you see, though an exceptional one. What I cannot understand is its writer. Why leave it blank? But what makes it worse, we know exactly who wrote it. Now, you will not leave me without further attention." @@P0011@@ I put this riddle subconsciously and added another element to it, from having happened to pick up the seedy black hat. @@P0012@@ "Something is missing here," said I. @@P0013@@ "Exactly the point: the rest of the note." @@P0014@@ "Then what is there missing if it's not there?" @@P0015@@ "I cannot say more on this point," said he. "Until you can show me one indisputably valuable piece of blue carbuncle or ornament of personal property, such trifles may expect to go neglected." @@P0020@@ The warmth of Holmes's sarcasm amused me, and satisfied that, very unlikely, anything finally conclusive would show, I drew myself aside and let his capricious but extremely easy manner run upon other and more important topics of conversation.
@@P0000@@ On glancing over my notes of the seventy odd cases in which I have during the last eight years studied the methods of my friend Sherlock Holmes, I find many tragic, some comic, a large number merely strange, but none commonplace; for, working as he did rather for the love of his art than for the acquirement of wealth, he refused to associate with any investigation which did not tend towards the unusual, and even the fantastic. @@P0001@@ Of all these varied cases, however, I cannot recall any which presented more singular features than that associated with the well-known Surrey family of the Roylotts of Stoke Moran. The events in question occurred in the early days of my association with Holmes, when we were sharing rooms as bachelors in Baker Street. It is possible that I might have placed them upon record before, but a promise of secrecy was made at the time, from which I have only been freed during the last month by the untimely death of the lady to whom the pledge was given. It is perhaps as well that the facts should now come to light, for I have reasons to know that there are widespread rumors as to the death of Dr. Grimesby Roylott which tend to make the matter even more terrible than the truth. @@P0002@@ It was early in April in the year '83 that I woke one morning to find Sherlock Holmes standing, fully dressed, by the side of my bed. He was a late riser, as a rule, and as the clock on the mantelpiece showed me that it was only a quarter-past seven, I blinked up at him in some surprise, and perhaps just a little resentment, for I was myself regular in my habits. @@P0003@@ "Very sorry to bother you, Watson," said he, "but it’s really worth it. There’s a client waiting in the sitting room who needs immediate advice. Her name is Helen Stoner, and she’s in a great deal of distress. You may be able to relieve her tension." @@P0004@@ "Then I shall have ample time to think about it over a cup of tea," I responded. "Certainly, though, get dressed and join us. She seems anxious," Holmes remarked. @@P0005@@ In a few minutes, I had given the matter some thought. After dressing quickly, I hastened into our sitting room. This is my intimate friend and associate, Dr. Watson, before whom you may speak as freely as you would to myself. Kindly tell us your story.”
@@P0000@@ Of all the cases that my friend, Mr. Sherlock Holmes, has been engaged in, there was only one where he refused to divulge any details to me. Now, with his permission, I can finally share it with the public, though in a more concise form than I might prefer. This is the case of the engineer’s thumb. @@P0001@@ It was during the summer of '89, a good while after our adventure with the Speckled Band, that I found myself alone in our rooms at Baker Street. Holmes had been absent all day, and I was beginning to wonder where he might be when the entrance door suddenly swung open. But Mr. Holmes is not here at the moment. Could I possibly assist you?" @@P0004@@ "I don’t think anyone but Mr. Holmes could help me," the young man replied. He collapsed into a chair and held out his hand. @@P0005@@ "I’ve had an accident," he said. @@P0006@@ I examined the outstretched hand, noting that the thumb was entirely missing. The wound was raw but surprisingly clean. @@P0007@@ "I must ask you to tell me your story," I said. "Otherwise, I can’t judge how I might assist you." @@P0008@@ "My name is Victor Hatherley," said he. "I’m a hydraulic engineer and had clean prospects until yesterday. The task offered a substantial fee, but Stark had insisted on utmost secrecy. @@P0010@@ "I was instructed to be at an address in the countryside by nightfall," Hatherley continued. "Once there, I was blindfolded and taken on a lengthy journey." @@P0011@@ Holmes returned just as this recount was ending. He paused in the doorway, his keen eyes taking in the scene. @@P0012@@ "This gentleman’s story intrigues me," said Holmes, as he settled into his chair. "Perhaps you can provide us with all the details, Mr. Hatherley." @@P0013@@ "Certainly," replied Hatherley. "When we arrived at our destination, I was shown a large hydraulic press, which appeared to be a new and complicated design. I was instructed to examine it." @@P0014@@ Hatherley described how Stark and his accomplice pressed him to judge the machine's capabilities, suspecting it might not run as purported. @@P0015@@ "As I complied, an alarming observation struck me, and I felt compelled to confront Stark." @@P0016@@ "What was your discovery?" inquired Holmes. @@P0017@@ "The machine was intended for no honest purpose—it was designed to forge coins!" @@P0018@@ Holmes nodded, clearly intrigued. @@P0019@@ "Things rapidly escalated," Hatherley continued. "Fearful of retribution, I decided to escape. In my haste, I inadvertently activated the press, injuring my hand. I narrowly avoided further harm." @@P0020@@ "The thumb?" asked Holmes. @@P0021@@ "I was attempting to shield my head from a blow when the press caught it," Hatherley explained. "I managed to free myself and fled for my life." @@P0022@@ Holmes and I exchanged a glance; the situation was indeed dire. Holmes asked a few more clarifying questions, his usual thoroughness evident. @@P0023@@ "Mr. Hatherley, could you locate this house again if needed?" Holmes finally asked. @@P0024@@ "I believe I could," Hatherley answered, "despite the blindfold. There were distinct sounds and smells during the journey." @@P0025@@ "Excellent," said Holmes. "We shall set out immediately." @@P0026@@ Our expedition led us through winding roads until we reached the secluded house. Holmes, in his meticulous fashion, scrutinized every detail. Yet, the building lay abandoned, with no sign of the machinery or its operators. @@P0027@@ A swift investigation confirmed our suspicions: the operation had moved on swiftly, leaving little evidence behind. Still, Holmes seemed content with the deductions derived from even the smallest clues. @@P0028@@ "This has been an intriguing case," Holmes remarked as we returned to Baker Street. "Fraught with danger yet illuminating about the lengths to which some men will go." @@P0029@@ I nodded, reflecting on the tenacity of the criminal mind, and the brilliance required to thwart it.

@@P0001@@ The day was a grim November one, and the dense fog pressed itself against the windows, cloaking the city in its shadow. Holmes sat beside the fire, flicking through the pages of one of those ponderous tomes, his mind seemingly disconnected from the world. At last, he turned his attention away from the pages and remarked, “You have a telegram there, Watson, open it up, and let’s see what delights await.” @@P0002@@ It was from Lord Robert St. Simon, a name of some notoriety in London. “Interesting,” Holmes noted, as he read it aloud. “He wishes to consult me on an urgent private matter. His presence here would prove more enlightening than mere correspondences.” @@P0003@@ An hour later, the noble bachelor himself graced our quarters. A genteel yet haughty man appeared before us, with his air both commanding and expectant. "Please, take a seat, my lord," Holmes gestured to the chair with an inviting nod. @@P0004@@ Lord St. Simon settled himself. "Mr. Holmes, I’ve found myself in an unprecedented predicament. After the ceremony, I presume?" @@P0006@@ "Yes, precisely. We celebrated in the grand house of my old friend, and with spirited confidence, I assumed all was well. Yet, prior to the wedding breakfast, she vanished entirely." @@P0007@@ Holmes nodded, absorbing each detail. "A delicate affair. Would you recount the events leading to her disappearance, as well as any conceivable causes you might perceive?" @@P0008@@ Lord St. Simon sighed, his frustration barely contained. "It's a torturous tangle. Miss Hatty Doran is an American heiress, her return to New York inevitable. Our engagement, while not without its trials, was amiable enough." @@P0009@@ Holmes leaned forward. "And was there anything unusual in her behavior leading up to the event?" @@P0010@@ "Not that I can pinpoint, besides a vague restlessness—a longing, perhaps, for the freedom of her former days." @@P0011@@ Holmes listened intently. "Such instincts can sometimes facilitate a yearning for escape." @@P0012@@ "There was one odd occurrence," Lord St. Simon continued. "A man, known to her past, appeared at the ceremony. His visit was unexplained—one could only suspect his intentions were less than noble." @@P0013@@ Holmes's eyes narrowed. "And did you recognize this gentleman?" @@P0014@@ "I did not, but he maintained a familiarity with my wife unmistakable to any observer." @@P0015@@ Holmes paused, contemplating. "I shall look into the matter with the utmost discretion. It would be best to conduct some inquiries of my own."
@@P0001@@ “Holmes,” I said, as I stood one morning looking through the window at the drab winter landscape, “these events are as inexplicable as they are grotesque. A week ago, you had a case involving a madman at St. Luke’s, and now this extraordinary affair has been thrust upon you. Has anything emerged? Is Watson required for consultation?” @@P0002@@ Holmes leaned back in his chair, the morning paper crumpled in his hands, and regarded me with an abstracted demeanor. “A true enigma, Watson,” he responded, “and intriguing because of its peculiarity. The beryl coronet represents a significant challenge. The more insights I gather, the more confounding the case becomes.” @@P0003@@ I joined Holmes by the fire. “Then you have some clues?” @@P0004@@ “Elizabeth Holder holds the key, Watson, but I’m awaiting a full picture. Patience, my friend. This conundrum demands a delicate hand, and a rash move might unravel the entire scheme.” @@P0005@@ At that very moment, the door swung open, and a gentleman of evident affluence entered. His face, marked by both anxiety and desperation, betrayed his emotions as he stood in the doorway. @@P0006@@ “I am at my wits’ end, Mr. Holmes. You must help me! @@P0001@@ To the man who loves art for its own sake, the world is full of beautiful things. It is to him that art will be least contaminated by any admixture of private motives. @@P0000@@ To Sherlock Holmes, she was always the woman. Rarely do I hear him mention her under any other name. In his eyes, she eclipses and predominates the whole of her sex. It was not that he felt any emotion akin to love for Irene Adler. All emotions, and that one particularly, were abhorrent to his cold, precise but admirably balanced mind. He was, I take it, the most perfect reasoning and observing machine that the world has seen; but as a lover, he would have placed himself in a false position. He never spoke of the softer passions, save with a gibe and a sneer. They were admirable things for the observer—excellent for drawing the veil from men's motives and actions. @@P0000@@ Boscombe Valley is a rural district near Ross, Herefordshire. The largest landowner there is Mr. John Turner, who made his fortune in Australia and returned to England some years ago. One of his farms, Hatherley, was rented by Mr. Charles McCarthy, also an ex-Australian. The two men had known each other in the colonies, so it was natural that they would settle as close to each other as possible. Turner appeared to be the wealthier man, so McCarthy became his tenant, yet they remained on equal terms, often seen together. McCarthy had one son, an eighteen-year-old, and Turner had a daughter of the same age, but neither had a living wife. They seemed to avoid the company of neighboring English families and led rather secluded lives, although both McCarthys enjoyed sports and were often seen at local race meetings. @@P0000@@ On June 3rd—last Monday—McCarthy left his house in Hatherley around three in the afternoon and walked to Boscombe Pool, a small lake where the stream through Boscombe Valley widens.
